People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

By the time I get through teaching and a department meeting, my body feels so heavy. Even making dinner or answering a few parent emails feels like climbing a hill.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t heard voices or seen things that other people couldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to, or do you find yourself fidgeting or restless more often?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed fidgeting more than usual. Maybe I move a little slower when I’m tired, but I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel guilty a lot. If a lesson doesn’t go well, or a student looks confused, I immediately think it’s my fault and that I’m letting everyone down. I’ll replay it over and over and tell myself I’m a terrible teacher… and then it kind of spills over into feeling like I’m a bad mother too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've been moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower. Maybe when I’m tired I take longer to get going, but I can’t say it’s been a clear change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never felt like someone else was controlling my thoughts or my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past year and seven months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t really weigh myself much, but my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. I haven’t been trying to lose weight. Most school days I just don’t eat until late afternoon, either I forget or I just avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Not really. I haven’t noticed myself being restless or unable to sit still. If anything, I just feel kind of worn out, but not like I’m pacing or fidgeting all the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low, would you say it's been constant over the past year and seven months or have there been periods where you felt better?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a year and seven months now. I think it started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. It hasn’t really felt like I’ve properly gone back to normal since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past year and seven months where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: No, not really. There might have been the odd day that was a bit easier, but I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt completely back to normal. It’s been going on since around when I took on the STEM assessment redesign along with my usual teaching work.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past year and seven months where you woke up feeling okay, like your normal self, and the low feelings didn't start until something happened during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. It’s more like it’s just been there most of the time for about a year and seven months, since I took on that STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching workload.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign, on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you enjoy doing or find pleasurable over the past year and seven months?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to really look forward to things like softball, my book club, working in my garden, and helping with robotics club. Now I either cancel or I show up and just kind of go through the motions. It doesn’t feel enjoyable the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in things like eating or sex?\n\nPatient: Yeah… in general, things just don’t feel enjoyable anymore. Like I said, I used to look forward to softball, book club, gardening, robotics club, and now I usually cancel or I’m there but just going through the motions. It’s like the pleasure part is kind of gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely my energy is lower. By the time I get through teaching and a department meeting, my body feels so heavy. I don’t think I’ve noticed fidgeting more than usual. Maybe I move a little slower when I’m tired, but I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel guilty a lot. If a lesson doesn’t go well, or a student looks confused, I immediately think it’s my fault and that I’m letting everyone down.
